        {"@context":"https://schema.org","@type":"CollectionPage","breadcrumb":{"@type":"BreadcrumbList","itemListElement":[{"@type":"ListItem","position":1,"item":{"@type":"WebPage","@id":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/","name":"热门内容"}}]},"description":"浏览来自职场专家的热门领英productivity内容。","hasPart":[{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"In companies where productivity has increased by 50%, creativity has doubled, and employee satisfaction is at an all-time high, one surprising change stands out: ditching the outdated obsession with time tracking.\n\nToo many managers are stuck in an outdated paradigm, fixating on:\n• When employees clock in\n• How long they sit at their desks\n• Micromanaging daily schedules\n\nBut we’ve hired smart, capable professionals. Treating them like children who need constant supervision is not just demeaning – it's counterproductive.\n\nHowever, it's crucial to maintain a balance. While micromanagement is detrimental, companies still need to ensure discipline and focus on key priorities. The goal is to empower employees while aligning their efforts with organizational objectives.\n\nThat’s why one needs to focus on result-focused management:\n\n1. Shift your metrics:\nFocus on project milestones, work quality, and client satisfaction instead of hours logged.\n\n2. Embrace flexibility:\nAllow flexible hours and remote work when possible. Trust employees to manage their time effectively.\n\n3. Cultivate a culture of trust:\nCommunicate openly about priorities and challenges. Reward results, not face time. Promote work-life balance and well-being.\n\nCompanies like Netflix, Basecamp, and Atlassian have implemented results-only work environments (ROWE) with remarkable success. \n\nThey report higher employee engagement, better outcomes, and a more dynamic, innovative workplace culture.\n\nWhat's one positive outcome you've experienced (as a manager or employee) when given more autonomy at work?\n\n#Leadership #EmployeeEmpowerment #WorkplaceCulture","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C4D03AQH9Hq6dwJF42g/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1656310773354?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=JPxCLBrF7FjykWJmZHW8ohPvQTz1gxPmQFdB_iegcjY"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":192823},"name":"Neha K Puri","url":"https://in.linkedin.com/in/nehakpuri"},"datePublished":"2024-07-16T04:34:47.705Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C4D03AQH9Hq6dwJF42g/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1656310773354?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=JPxCLBrF7FjykWJmZHW8ohPvQTz1gxPmQFdB_iegcjY"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":93616},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":1,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/nehakpuri_in-companies-where-productivity-has-increased-activity-7218835437371420673-mLki"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"It’s simple math 🧐\n\nI use to think that motivation was the key to monumental success. Long story short, it’s not. It’s about the little things you do every day that will take you from reasonable to slightly unreasonable to completely unreasonable progress. \n\nYour future is not defined by how motivated you are, but by your daily routines and systems. \n\nI believe in this so much that we named our company  Butterfly 3ffect  to reflect the value of incremental gains. \n\nwe believe that that’s how the best people and brands grow. \n\nHere’s how you grow the small way: \n\n\n1. Start by setting achievable goals, like reading one chapter of a book each day or going for a short walk\n\n2. Practice gratitude by writing down three things you're thankful for every night before bed\n\n3. Engage in daily self-reflection, even if it's just for a few minutes, to assess your thoughts and actions\n\n4. Incorporate small acts of kindness into your daily routine, like holding the door for someone or offering a genuine compliment\n\n5. Learn something new every day, whether it's a fun fact, a new word, or a new skill\n\n6. Prioritise self-care by getting enough sleep, staying hydrated, and taking breaks when needed\n\n7. Surround yourself with positive influences, whether it's uplifting books, supportive friends, or inspiring podcasts\n\n8. Embrace failure as a learning opportunity and a stepping stone to growth\n\n9. Stay consistent and patient, knowing that small progress over time adds up to significant improvement\n\n10. Celebrate your achievements, no matter how small, to stay motivated and encouraged along the way.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGCOaRywrVQdg/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B4EZ4v349cJMAc-/0/1778919643352?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=dtS4otm47Fgg4sGl9Ndzo1H-9CANRvCX8qKNQpbdwdk"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":421170},"name":"Elfried Samba","url":"https://uk.linkedin.com/in/elfriedsamba"},"datePublished":"2024-02-15T12:30:29.134Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQGCOaRywrVQdg/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B4EZ4v349cJMAc-/0/1778919643352?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=dtS4otm47Fgg4sGl9Ndzo1H-9CANRvCX8qKNQpbdwdk"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":32287},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":2,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/elfriedsamba_its-simple-math-i-use-to-think-that-activity-7163872193196580864-Bfs4"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"The silent productivity killer you've never heard of...\n\nAttention Residue (and 3 strategies to fight back):\n\nThe concept of \"attention residue\" was first identified by University of Washington business professor Dr. Sophie Leroy in 2009.\n\nThe idea is quite simple:\n\nThere is a cognitive cost to shifting your attention from one task to another. When our attention is shifted, there is a \"residue\" that remains in the brain and impairs our cognitive performance on the new task.\n\nPut differently, you may think your attention has fully shifted to the next task, but your brain has a lag—it thinks otherwise!\n\nIt's relatively easy to find examples of this effect in your own life:\n\n• You get on a call but are still thinking about the prior call.\n• An email pops up during meeting and derails your focus.\n• You check your phone during a lecture and can't refocus afterwards.\n\nThere are two key points worth noting here:\n\n1. The research indicates it doesn't seem to matter whether the task switch is \"macro\" (i.e. moving from one major task to the next) or \"micro\" (i.e. pausing one major task for a quick check on some minor task).\n\n2. The challenge is even more pronounced in a remote/hybrid world, where we're free to roam the internet, have our chat apps open, and check our phones all while appearing to be focused in a Zoom meeting.\n\nWith apologies to any self-proclaimed proficient multitaskers, the research is very clear:\n\nEvery single time you call upon your brain to move away from one task and toward another, you are hurting its performance—your work quality and efficiency suffer.\n\nAuthor Cal Newport puts it well:\n\n\"If, like most, you rarely go more than 10–15 minutes without a just check, you have effectively put yourself in a persistent state of self-imposed cognitive handicap.\"\n\nHere are three strategies to manage attention residue and fight back:\n\n1. Focus Work Blocks: Block time on your calendar for sprints of focused energy. Set a timer for a 45-90 minute window, close everything except the task at hand, and focus on one thing. It works wonders.\n\n2. Take a Breather: Whenever possible, create open windows of 5-15 minutes between higher value tasks. Schedule 25-minute calls. Block those windows on your calendar. During them, take a walk or close your eyes and breathe.\n\n3. Batch Processing: You still have to reply to messages and emails. Pick a few windows during the day when you will deeply focus on the task of processing and replying to these. Your response quality will go up from this batching, and they won't bleed into the rest of your day.\n\nAttention residue is a silent killer of your work quality and efficiency.\n\nUnderstanding it—and taking the steps to fight back—will have an immediate positive impact on your work and life.\n\nIf you enjoyed this or learned something, share it with others and follow me Sahil Bloom for more in future! The beautiful visualization is by Roberto Ferraro.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQFdgn2OU81oKQ/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1667155655120?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=Al8LEhBtmwB9aj3dneywa83zg4Cmi20RG5sKZExGLUM"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":720997},"name":"Sahil Bloom","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/sahilbloom"},"datePublished":"2023-12-10T12:45:09.369Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQFdgn2OU81oKQ/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1667155655120?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=Al8LEhBtmwB9aj3dneywa83zg4Cmi20RG5sKZExGLUM"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":19516},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":3,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/sahilbloom_the-silent-productivity-killer-youve-never-activity-7139595898145107968-Kes-"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Do you feel guilty about taking time off?\n\nI used to spend weekends, trips, and lunch breaks (!!) terrified that I was falling behind. I had to constantly fight the compulsion to get back to my inbox.\n\nNow I remind myself: Your mental health is the foundation for your ability to do great work.\n\nWe often think of vacations or breaks as rewards we need to earn. This is backward thinking. Your wellbeing is what allows you to achieve your goals. A successful career depends on you having rested enough to be creative, show up for others, and make good decisions.\n\nIt sounds obvious but it bears repeating: When you fail to take the time you need to recharge, you set yourself up to fail.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4D03AQEc7EVbvsuQrQ/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1649195396417?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=q7ANPKvRdhMAR8_h-Iaq2qloEH6SKgUuLv4fiAz1xMY"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":188555},"name":"Liz Fosslien","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/liz-fosslien"},"datePublished":"2023-08-01T14:30:01.933Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4D03AQEc7EVbvsuQrQ/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1649195396417?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=q7ANPKvRdhMAR8_h-Iaq2qloEH6SKgUuLv4fiAz1xMY"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":6852},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":4,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/liz-fosslien_do-you-feel-guilty-about-taking-time-off-activity-7092149480619216897-jjeL"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"I built an AI agent that handles my entire inbound system. (And I used to be against automation). Here's how I did it:\n\nI used two tools:\n--\u003E Make: For automation workflows\n--\u003E Relevance: For AI agents\n\nHere's what my AI agent handles:\n\nWhen someone fills our form, it-\n--\u003E Analyzes their LinkedIn profile\n--\u003E Reviews their website\n--\u003E Checks if they match our criteria\n--\u003E Makes a decision in seconds\n\nFor qualified leads:\n--\u003E Sends personalized pitch deck\n--\u003E Books discovery calls\n--\u003E Handles initial questions\n\nFor non-qualified leads:\n--\u003E Sends a thoughtful rejection\n--\u003E Explains why we're not the right fit\n--\u003E Keeps the door open for future\nThe best part?\n\nMy team and I can focus on what matters - strategy and client success - instead of spending hours on admin work.\n\nNo more:\n-Manual lead checking\n-Back-and-forth emails\n-Calendar scheduling headaches\n-Just high-quality conversations with pre-qualified founders.\n\nWant to know the biggest lesson?\n\nAutomation isn't about replacing the human touch. It's about creating more time for it.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D5603AQG5H5cJMoT53g/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B56ZvvKD5JKcAY-/0/1769243955246?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=3nnRr_dJ3ji7eEGKgO2Bbd7fAQCP9_vAmFU-2oRH8z8"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":321886},"name":"Vedika Bhaia","url":"https://in.linkedin.com/in/vedikabhaia"},"datePublished":"2025-01-23T11:31:19.324Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D5603AQG5H5cJMoT53g/profile-displayphoto-scale_200_200/B56ZvvKD5JKcAY-/0/1769243955246?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=3nnRr_dJ3ji7eEGKgO2Bbd7fAQCP9_vAmFU-2oRH8z8"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":798},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":5,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/vedikabhaia_i-built-an-ai-agent-that-handles-my-entire-activity-7288156342152814592-VYJg"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Want to stay motivated every single day?\n\nBorrow a strategy from Harvard.\n\nThen borrow another from stand up comedy.\n\nTogether, they’re a powerhouse for momentum, motivation, and mastery.\n\nHere’s how it works:\n\nLet’s start with Harvard.\n\nResearcher Teresa Amabile studied 12,000 daily work diaries across 8 companies.\n\nShe wanted to know: What truly motivates people on a day to day basis?\n\nWhat she found changed how we understand drive.\n\nThe #1 driver of daily motivation wasn’t:\n\nMoney\nPraise\nPerks\n\nIt was progress.\n\nThe days people made progress on meaningful work were the days they felt the best.\n\nProgress isn’t a luxury. It’s a psychological necessity.\n\nSo how do we make progress feel visible especially on days when it’s not?\n\nUse a “Progress Ritual.”\n\n→ At the end of the day, pause. → Write down 3 small ways you moved forward. → That’s it.\nNo fanfare. Just ritual.\nThis works because we rarely notice our progress in real time.\n\nIt gets buried under busyness, meetings, and mental noise.\n\nThe act of looking back gives your brain the reward it needs to keep going.\n\nMomentum builds from meaning.\n\nNow let’s add some comedy.\n\nYoung Jerry Seinfeld had one goal: write new material every day.\n\nTo stay on track, he created a brilliant system.\n\nEach day he wrote, he put a big red X on his calendar.\n\nSoon, a chain of Xs formed.\n\nAnd here’s the key:\n\nDon’t break the chain.\n\nOne red X becomes two. Two becomes ten. Ten becomes identity.\n\nWhether you’re writing, coding, or training\n\nDaily action + visual chain = long-term motivation.\nSummary: The Two-Part Motivation System\nFrom Harvard: Record 3 ways you made progress each day. From Seinfeld: Mark an X for each day you show up then don’t break the chain.\n\nProgress fuels purpose. Consistency fuels confidence.\n\nApply both and you’ll stay on track especially on the tough days.\n\nBecause when your days get better, your weeks get better. When your weeks get better, your months get better. When your months get better, your life gets better.\n\nIt starts with one small win today.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C4D03AQFb3nNCAlB-tA/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1643045852847?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=J6mpVxbZ2gS5TNt4ea1PUMv0yuFa-X-qMUzIUKKcP5Y"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":444547},"name":"Daniel Pink","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/danielpink"},"datePublished":"2025-08-20T15:50:57.951Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C4D03AQFb3nNCAlB-tA/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1643045852847?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=J6mpVxbZ2gS5TNt4ea1PUMv0yuFa-X-qMUzIUKKcP5Y"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":4177},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":6,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/danielpink_want-to-stay-motivated-every-single-day-activity-7363960747510382593--BUO"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Few Lessons from Deploying and Using LLMs in Production\n\nDeploying LLMs can feel like hiring a hyperactive genius intern—they dazzle users while potentially draining your API budget. Here are some insights I’ve gathered:\n\n1. “Cheap” is a Lie You Tell Yourself: Cloud costs per call may seem low, but the overall expense of an LLM-based system can skyrocket.\n\nFixes: \n- Cache repetitive queries: Users ask the same thing at least 100x/day\n- Gatekeep: Use cheap classifiers (BERT) to filter “easy” requests. Let LLMs handle only the complex 10% and your current systems handle the remaining 90%.\n- Quantize your models: Shrink LLMs to run on cheaper hardware without massive accuracy drops\n- Asynchronously build your caches — Pre-generate common responses before they’re requested or gracefully fail the first time a query comes and cache for the next time.\n\n2. Guard Against Model Hallucinations: Sometimes, models express answers with such confidence that distinguishing fact from fiction becomes challenging, even for human reviewers.\n\nFixes: \n- Use RAG - Just a fancy way of saying to provide your model the knowledge it requires in the prompt itself by querying some database based on semantic matches with the query. \n- Guardrails: Validate outputs using regex or cross-encoders to establish a clear decision boundary between the query and the LLM’s response.\n\n3. The best LLM is often a discriminative model: You don’t always need a full LLM. Consider knowledge distillation: use a large LLM to label your data and then train a smaller, discriminative model that performs similarly at a much lower cost.\n\n4. It's not about the model, it is about the data on which it is trained: A smaller LLM might struggle with specialized domain data—that’s normal. Fine-tune your model on your specific data set by starting with parameter-efficient methods (like LoRA or Adapters) and using synthetic data generation to bootstrap training.\n\n5. Prompts are the new Features: Prompts are the new features in your system. Version them, run A/B tests, and continuously refine using online experiments. Consider bandit algorithms to automatically promote the best-performing variants.\n\nWhat do you think? Have I missed anything? I’d love to hear your “I survived LLM prod” stories in the comments!","author":{"@type":"Person","image":"","interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":46160},"name":"Rahul Agarwal","url":"https://uk.linkedin.com/in/rahulagwl"},"datePublished":"2025-04-11T03:30:02.380Z","image":"","interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":1169},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":7,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/rahulagwl_few-lessons-from-deploying-and-using-llms-activity-7316301476941701120-BxFr"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"The day I took a 3 AM business call from New Zealand, I knew I'd failed.\n\nMy girlfriend looked at me and said: \"You don't own a business. Your business owns you.\"\n\nShe was right.\n\nI'd built a successful company but forgot to build myself out of it.\n\nThe best founders do one thing brilliantly: they build systems.\n\nHere's the framework that changed everything:\n\n1. The $5,000 Hour Rule\n\nI audit my week every Sunday with one question: \n\nHow many hours were actually worth $5,000?\n\n$5,000 hour tasks:\n\n• Vision setting\n• Hiring A-players\n• Strategic decisions\n• High-level partnerships\n\n$10 hour tasks:\n\n• Inbox zero\n• Editing videos\n• Micromanaging\n• Managing Slack threads\n\nThen I delegate, automate, or eliminate everything else.\n\n2. The 4 Core Systems\n\nMost founders get lost in complexity.\n\nI focus on four systems:\n\n1. Vision Clarity System\n\nWhere are we going in 3 years? Everyone needs to know.\n\n2. Role Definition System\n\nWho owns what? No overlap. No confusion.\n\n3. Communication Rhythm System\n\nHow do we stay aligned without endless meetings?\n\n4. Decision Framework System \n\nHow do we choose fast without me being involved?\n\nBuild these four first. Everything else is noise.\n\n3. Hire Solutions, Not Problems\n\nThe worst hires ask: \"Matt, what should I do?\"\n\nThe best hires say: \"Here's the problem, here are 3 solutions, here's my recommendation.\"\n\nThis one shift let me go from 16-hour days to 4-hour days.\n\n4. The Rule of Three\n\nAnything I do more than 3 times gets documented.\n\nLoom plus Google Docs create playbooks so detailed a high schooler could follow them.\n\nClient onboarding? Documented.\n\nTeam meetings? Documented.\n\nCustomer complaints? Documented.\n\nIf it's not documented, it doesn't exist in my company.\n\n5. The Freedom Test\n\nHere's how you know your systems work: \n\nCan you disappear for 4 weeks without your business falling apart?\n\nI recently spent 3 weeks in the Dolomites with zero business calls.\n\nMy team made every decision.\n\nRevenue grew 12%.\n\nCustomers were happier.\n\nThat's what real systems look like.\n\nMost founders build themselves into their business.\n\nSmart founders build themselves out of it.\n\n__\n\nEnjoy this? ♻️ Repost it to your network and follow Matt Gray for more.\n\nReady to learn how to remove yourself from operations?\n\nGet the complete system that helps founders multiply profits while working less: https://lnkd.in/eQ4RCByh","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQE3vK-6anIV3Q/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1686793511383?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=O3jU6sCqnQ8Dm2Yi5hcIgH2bXJeayToh8Q24p80FbzI"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":923226},"name":"Matt Gray","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/mattgray1"},"datePublished":"2025-08-22T11:59:56.262Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4E03AQE3vK-6anIV3Q/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1686793511383?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=O3jU6sCqnQ8Dm2Yi5hcIgH2bXJeayToh8Q24p80FbzI"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":1801},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":8,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/mattgray1_the-best-founders-do-one-thing-activity-7364627383120711680-gmmf"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"Just out in Harvard Business Review, summary of the Hybrid Experiment results and lessons on how to make hybrid succeed.\n\nExperiment: randomize 1600 graduate employees in marketing, finance, accounting and engineering at Trip.com into 5-days a week in office, or 3-days a week in office and 2-days a week WFH. Analyzed 2 years of data.\n\nTwo key results\nA)  Hybrid and fully-in-office showed no differences in productivity, performance review grade, promotion, learning or innovation.\nB) Hybrid had a higher satisfaction rate, and 35% lower attrition. Quit-rate reductions were largest for female employees.\n\nFour managerial lessons\n1) Hybrid needs a strong performance management system so managers don’t need to hover over employees at their desks to check their progress. Trip.com had an extensive performance review process every six months.\n\n2) Coordinate in-office days at the team or company level. Schedule clarity prevents the frustration of coming to an empty office only to participate in Zoom calls. Trip.com coordinated WFH on Wednesday and Friday. \n\n3) Having leadership buy-in is critical (as with most management practices). Trip.com’s CEO and C-suite all support the hybrid policy. \n\n4) A/B test new policies (as well as products) if possible. Often new policies turn out to be unexpectedly profitable. Trip.com made millions of dollars more profits from hybrid by cutting expensive turnover.","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C5603AQGO7_73qKwDxQ/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1659481493311?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=yqWvgJvpKzeIkxh1BOgxfIuHdyZtLYLdIM1nWxQLCiM"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":77243},"name":"Nick Bloom","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/in/nick-bloom-stanford"},"datePublished":"2024-10-29T13:16:25.918Z","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/C5603AQGO7_73qKwDxQ/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/0/1659481493311?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=yqWvgJvpKzeIkxh1BOgxfIuHdyZtLYLdIM1nWxQLCiM"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"http://schema.org/LikeAction","userInteractionCount":9057},"isPartOf":{"@type":"CollectionPage","name":"Productivity","url":"https://www.linkedin.com/top-content/productivity/"},"position":9,"publisher":{"@type":"Organization","name":"LinkedIn"},"sharedContent":{"@type":"WebPage"},"url":"https://www.linkedin.com/posts/nick-bloom-stanford_just-out-in-harvard-business-review-summary-activity-7257017437437468673-6Nkh"},{"@type":"SocialMediaPosting","articleBody":"A few years ago, I was in a high stakes meeting with colleagues from Japan.\nI presented my points confidently, thinking I was making a great impression.\nBut as I scanned the room, I saw blank expressions. \n\nNo nods. No engagement. Just silence.\n\nI panicked. Had I said something wrong? Was my idea unconvincing?\n\nAfter the meeting, one of my Japanese colleagues pulled me aside and said, “Sumit, we really want to understand you, but you speak too fast.”\n\nThat was my light bulb moment.\n\nFor years, I assumed that mastering English and business communication was enough to build strong global relationships.\n\nBut the real challenge wasn’t just the language - it was the rate of speech!\nMost of us don’t realize that speaking speed varies drastically across cultures. \n\nHere’s an eye-opener:\n·      In India, we typically speak at 120–150 words per minute.\n·      The global standard for clear communication is around 60–80 words per minute.\n·      In Japan, where English is not the first language, this rate drops even further.\n\nSo, what happens when we, as fast speakers, communicate with someone who is used to a much slower pace?\n\nOur words blur together. The listener struggles to process. And instead of making an impact, we create confusion.\n\nWe often assume that if people don’t understand us, we need to repeat ourselves.\n\nBut the truth is, we don’t need to repeat - we need to slow down, simplify, and pause.\n\nIf you work in a multicultural environment, here are three things that can dramatically improve your communication:\na.   Control your pace: Consciously slow down when speaking to an international audience. What feels “normal” to you might be too fast for them.\nb.   Use simple language: Smaller sentences. Easier words (vocabulary).\nc.    Pause & check for understanding: Don’t assume silence means agreement. Ask, “Does that make sense?” or “Would you like me to clarify anything?”\n\nI’ve seen professionals struggle in global roles - not because they lack expertise, but because they fail to adjust their communication style to their audience.\n\nI’ve also seen leaders who thrive across cultures, simply because they master the art of respectful, clear, and paced communication.\n\nIf you want to succeed in a global workplace, rate of speech is not just a skill - it’s a strategy.\n\nHave you ever faced challenges due to differences in speaking speed? Let’s discuss.\n#GlobalCommunication #CrossCulturalLeadership #EffectiveCommunication #SoftSkills #CareerGrowth #WorkplaceSuccess #HR","author":{"@type":"Person","image":{"@type":"ImageObject","url":"https://media.licdn.com/dms/image/v2/D4D03AQGDgmHDV2jsrg/profile-displayphoto-shrink_200_200/B4DZTUSQH2HIAc-/0/1738728342640?e=2147483647&v=beta&t=vSg5W8QIYEORZmchpshHmqGNKYuI_NdRL-cEtnQHaao"},"interactionStatistic":{"@type":"InteractionCounter","interactionType":"https://schema.org/FollowAction","userInteractionCount":51997},"name":"Sumit   <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">In companies where productivity has increased by 50%, creativity has doubled, and employee satisfaction is at an all-time high, one surprising change stands out: ditching the outdated obsession with time tracking.

Too many managers are stuck in an outdated paradigm, fixating on:
• When employees clock in
• How long they sit at their desks
• Micromanaging daily schedules

But we’ve hired smart, capable professionals. Treating them like children who need constant supervision is not just demeaning – it's counterproductive.

However, it's crucial to maintain a balance. While micromanagement is detrimental, companies still need to ensure discipline and focus on key priorities. The goal is to empower employees while aligning their efforts with organizational objectives.

That’s why one needs to focus on result-focused management:

1. Shift your metrics:
Focus on project milestones, work quality, and client satisfaction instead of hours logged.

2. Embrace flexibility:
Allow flexible hours and remote work when possible. Trust employees to manage their time effectively.

3. Cultivate a culture of trust:
Communicate openly about priorities and challenges. Reward results, not face time. Promote work-life balance and well-being.

Companies like <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/netflix?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Netflix</a>, <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/company/basecamp6?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Basecamp</a>, and <a class="link" href="https://au.linkedin.com/company/atlassian?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Atlassian</a> have implemented results-only work environments (ROWE) with remarkable success. 

They report higher employee engagement, better outcomes, and a more dynamic, innovative workplace culture.

What's one positive outcome you've experienced (as a manager or employee) when given more autonomy at work?

<a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fleadership&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#Leadership</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Femployeeempowerment&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#EmployeeEmpowerment</a> <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Ffeed%2Fhashtag%2Fworkplaceculture&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>#WorkplaceCulture</a>
</p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">It’s simple math 🧐

I use to think that motivation was the key to monumental success. Long story short, it’s not. It’s about the little things you do every day that will take you from reasonable to slightly unreasonable to completely unreasonable progress. 

Your future is not defined by how motivated you are, but by your daily routines and systems. 

I believe in this so much that we named our company  <a class="link" href="https://uk.linkedin.com/company/butterflyeffectxyz?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Butterfly 3ffect</a>  to reflect the value of incremental gains. 

we believe that that’s how the best people and brands grow. 

Here’s how you grow the small way: 


1. Start by setting achievable goals, like reading one chapter of a book each day or going for a short walk

2. Practice gratitude by writing down three things you're thankful for every night before bed

3. Engage in daily self-reflection, even if it's just for a few minutes, to assess your thoughts and actions

4. Incorporate small acts of kindness into your daily routine, like holding the door for someone or offering a genuine compliment

5. Learn something new every day, whether it's a fun fact, a new word, or a new skill

6. Prioritise self-care by getting enough sleep, staying hydrated, and taking breaks when needed

7. Surround yourself with positive influences, whether it's uplifting books, supportive friends, or inspiring podcasts

8. Embrace failure as a learning opportunity and a stepping stone to growth

9. Stay consistent and patient, knowing that small progress over time adds up to significant improvement

10. Celebrate your achievements, no matter how small, to stay motivated and encouraged along the way.</p>

      …展开
    </button>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">The silent productivity killer you've never heard of...

Attention Residue (and 3 strategies to fight back):

The concept of "attention residue" was first identified by University of Washington business professor Dr. Sophie Leroy in 2009.

The idea is quite simple:

There is a cognitive cost to shifting your attention from one task to another. When our attention is shifted, there is a "residue" that remains in the brain and impairs our cognitive performance on the new task.

Put differently, you may think your attention has fully shifted to the next task, but your brain has a lag—it thinks otherwise!

It's relatively easy to find examples of this effect in your own life:

• You get on a call but are still thinking about the prior call.
• An email pops up during meeting and derails your focus.
• You check your phone during a lecture and can't refocus afterwards.

There are two key points worth noting here:

1. The research indicates it doesn't seem to matter whether the task switch is "macro" (i.e. moving from one major task to the next) or "micro" (i.e. pausing one major task for a quick check on some minor task).

2. The challenge is even more pronounced in a remote/hybrid world, where we're free to roam the internet, have our chat apps open, and check our phones all while appearing to be focused in a Zoom meeting.

With apologies to any self-proclaimed proficient multitaskers, the research is very clear:

Every single time you call upon your brain to move away from one task and toward another, you are hurting its performance—your work quality and efficiency suffer.

Author Cal Newport puts it well:

"If, like most, you rarely go more than 10–15 minutes without a just check, you have effectively put yourself in a persistent state of self-imposed cognitive handicap."

Here are three strategies to manage attention residue and fight back:

1. Focus Work Blocks: Block time on your calendar for sprints of focused energy. Set a timer for a 45-90 minute window, close everything except the task at hand, and focus on one thing. It works wonders.

2. Take a Breather: Whenever possible, create open windows of 5-15 minutes between higher value tasks. Schedule 25-minute calls. Block those windows on your calendar. During them, take a walk or close your eyes and breathe.

3. Batch Processing: You still have to reply to messages and emails. Pick a few windows during the day when you will deeply focus on the task of processing and replying to these. Your response quality will go up from this batching, and they won't bleed into the rest of your day.

Attention residue is a silent killer of your work quality and efficiency.

Understanding it—and taking the steps to fight back—will have an immediate positive impact on your work and life.

If you enjoyed this or learned something, share it with others and follow me <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sahilbloom?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Sahil Bloom</a> for more in future! The beautiful visualization is by <a class="link" href="https://es.linkedin.com/in/ferraroroberto?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Roberto Ferraro</a>.</p>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Do you feel guilty about taking time off?

I used to spend weekends, trips, and lunch breaks (!!) terrified that I was falling behind. I had to constantly fight the compulsion to get back to my inbox.

Now I remind myself: Your mental health is the foundation for your ability to do great work.

We often think of vacations or breaks as rewards we need to earn. This is backward thinking. Your wellbeing is what allows you to achieve your goals. A successful career depends on you having rested enough to be creative, show up for others, and make good decisions.

It sounds obvious but it bears repeating: When you fail to take the time you need to recharge, you set yourself up to fail.</p>

      …展开
    </button>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">I built an AI agent that handles my entire inbound system. (And I used to be against automation). Here's how I did it:

I used two tools:
--&gt; Make: For automation workflows
--&gt; Relevance: For AI agents

Here's what my AI agent handles:

When someone fills our form, it-
--&gt; Analyzes their LinkedIn profile
--&gt; Reviews their website
--&gt; Checks if they match our criteria
--&gt; Makes a decision in seconds

For qualified leads:
--&gt; Sends personalized pitch deck
--&gt; Books discovery calls
--&gt; Handles initial questions

For non-qualified leads:
--&gt; Sends a thoughtful rejection
--&gt; Explains why we're not the right fit
--&gt; Keeps the door open for future
The best part?

My team and I can focus on what matters - strategy and client success - instead of spending hours on admin work.

No more:
-Manual lead checking
-Back-and-forth emails
-Calendar scheduling headaches
-Just high-quality conversations with pre-qualified founders.

Want to know the biggest lesson?

Automation isn't about replacing the human touch. It's about creating more time for it.</p>

      …展开
    </button>

    <a href="/uas/login?session_redirect=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linkedin.com%2Fposts%2Fdanielpink_want-to-stay-motivated-every-single-day-activity-7363960747510382593--BUO&amp;trk=keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect&amp;guestReportContentType=POST&amp;_f=guest-reporting" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore-sign-in-redirect" data-tracking-will-navigate data-item-type="semaphore" data-semaphore-content-type="POST" data-semaphore-content-urn="urn:li:activity:7363960747510382593" data-semaphore-tracking-prefix="keyword-landing-page_ellipsis-menu-semaphore" data-is-logged-in="false" data-modal="semaphore__toggle" class="semaphore__toggle visited:text-color-text-secondary ellipsis-menu__semaphore ellipsis-menu__item-button flex items-center w-full p-1 cursor-pointer font-sans text-sm font-bold link-styled focus:link-styled link:no-underline active:bg-color-background-container-tint focus:bg-color-background-container-tint hover:bg-color-background-container-tint outline-offset-[-2px]" role="menuitem">
<!---->        
                      <icon class="ellipsis-menu__item-icon text-color-text h-[24px] w-[24px] mr-1" data-delayed-url="https://static.licdn.com/aero-v1/sc/h/iq0x9q37wj214o129ai1yjut">

                      举报此动态

      </div>

      
            
            
  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Want to stay motivated every single day?

Borrow a strategy from Harvard.

Then borrow another from stand up comedy.

Together, they’re a powerhouse for momentum, motivation, and mastery.

Here’s how it works:

Let’s start with Harvard.

Researcher Teresa Amabile studied 12,000 daily work diaries across 8 companies.

She wanted to know: What truly motivates people on a day to day basis?

What she found changed how we understand drive.

The #1 driver of daily motivation wasn’t:

Money
Praise
Perks

It was progress.

The days people made progress on meaningful work were the days they felt the best.

Progress isn’t a luxury. It’s a psychological necessity.

So how do we make progress feel visible especially on days when it’s not?

Use a “Progress Ritual.”

→ At the end of the day, pause. → Write down 3 small ways you moved forward. → That’s it.
No fanfare. Just ritual.
This works because we rarely notice our progress in real time.

It gets buried under busyness, meetings, and mental noise.

The act of looking back gives your brain the reward it needs to keep going.

Momentum builds from meaning.

Now let’s add some comedy.

Young Jerry Seinfeld had one goal: write new material every day.

To stay on track, he created a brilliant system.

Each day he wrote, he put a big red X on his calendar.

Soon, a chain of Xs formed.

And here’s the key:

Don’t break the chain.

One red X becomes two. Two becomes ten. Ten becomes identity.

Whether you’re writing, coding, or training

Daily action + visual chain = long-term motivation.
Summary: The Two-Part Motivation System
From Harvard: Record 3 ways you made progress each day. From Seinfeld: Mark an X for each day you show up then don’t break the chain.

Progress fuels purpose. Consistency fuels confidence.

Apply both and you’ll stay on track especially on the tough days.

Because when your days get better, your weeks get better. When your weeks get better, your months get better. When your months get better, your life gets better.

It starts with one small win today.</p>
          
            
                  
    

    <button class="attributed-text-segment-list__btn-truncation absolute right-0 bottom-0 pl-1 bg-color-background-container text-color-text-secondary text-sm font-medium babybear:font-bold invisible cursor-pointer hover:text-color-link-hover hover:underline" data-feed-action="see-more-post" data-feed-action-category="EXPAND" data-feed-action-type="expandCommentaryText" data-feed-control="commentary_expand" aria-hidden="true" tabindex="0">
      …展开
    </button>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Few Lessons from Deploying and Using LLMs in Production

Deploying LLMs can feel like hiring a hyperactive genius intern—they dazzle users while potentially draining your API budget. Here are some insights I’ve gathered:

1. “Cheap” is a Lie You Tell Yourself: Cloud costs per call may seem low, but the overall expense of an LLM-based system can skyrocket.

Fixes: 
- Cache repetitive queries: Users ask the same thing at least 100x/day
- Gatekeep: Use cheap classifiers (BERT) to filter “easy” requests. Let LLMs handle only the complex 10% and your current systems handle the remaining 90%.
- Quantize your models: Shrink LLMs to run on cheaper hardware without massive accuracy drops
- Asynchronously build your caches — Pre-generate common responses before they’re requested or gracefully fail the first time a query comes and cache for the next time.

2. Guard Against Model Hallucinations: Sometimes, models express answers with such confidence that distinguishing fact from fiction becomes challenging, even for human reviewers.

Fixes: 
- Use RAG - Just a fancy way of saying to provide your model the knowledge it requires in the prompt itself by querying some database based on semantic matches with the query. 
- Guardrails: Validate outputs using regex or cross-encoders to establish a clear decision boundary between the query and the LLM’s response.

3. The best LLM is often a discriminative model: You don’t always need a full LLM. Consider knowledge distillation: use a large LLM to label your data and then train a smaller, discriminative model that performs similarly at a much lower cost.

4. It's not about the model, it is about the data on which it is trained: A smaller LLM might struggle with specialized domain data—that’s normal. Fine-tune your model on your specific data set by starting with parameter-efficient methods (like LoRA or Adapters) and using synthetic data generation to bootstrap training.

5. Prompts are the new Features: Prompts are the new features in your system. Version them, run A/B tests, and continuously refine using online experiments. Consider bandit algorithms to automatically promote the best-performing variants.

What do you think? Have I missed anything? I’d love to hear your “I survived LLM prod” stories in the comments!</p>

      …展开
    </button>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">The day I took a 3 AM business call from New Zealand, I knew I'd failed.

My girlfriend looked at me and said: "You don't own a business. Your business owns you."

She was right.

I'd built a successful company but forgot to build myself out of it.

The best founders do one thing brilliantly: they build systems.

Here's the framework that changed everything:

1. The $5,000 Hour Rule

I audit my week every Sunday with one question: 

How many hours were actually worth $5,000?

$5,000 hour tasks:

• Vision setting
• Hiring A-players
• Strategic decisions
• High-level partnerships

$10 hour tasks:

• Inbox zero
• Editing videos
• Micromanaging
• Managing Slack threads

Then I delegate, automate, or eliminate everything else.

2. The 4 Core Systems

Most founders get lost in complexity.

I focus on four systems:

1. Vision Clarity System

Where are we going in 3 years? Everyone needs to know.

2. Role Definition System

Who owns what? No overlap. No confusion.

3. Communication Rhythm System

How do we stay aligned without endless meetings?

4. Decision Framework System 

How do we choose fast without me being involved?

Build these four first. Everything else is noise.

3. Hire Solutions, Not Problems

The worst hires ask: "Matt, what should I do?"

The best hires say: "Here's the problem, here are 3 solutions, here's my recommendation."

This one shift let me go from 16-hour days to 4-hour days.

4. The Rule of Three

Anything I do more than 3 times gets documented.

Loom plus Google Docs create playbooks so detailed a high schooler could follow them.

Client onboarding? Documented.

Team meetings? Documented.

Customer complaints? Documented.

If it's not documented, it doesn't exist in my company.

5. The Freedom Test

Here's how you know your systems work: 

Can you disappear for 4 weeks without your business falling apart?

I recently spent 3 weeks in the Dolomites with zero business calls.

My team made every decision.

Revenue grew 12%.

Customers were happier.

That's what real systems look like.

Most founders build themselves into their business.

Smart founders build themselves out of it.

__

Enjoy this? ♻️ Repost it to your network and follow <a class="link" href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/mattgray1?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Matt Gray</a> for more.

Ready to learn how to remove yourself from operations?

Get the complete system that helps founders multiply profits while working less: <a class="link" href="https://lnkd.in/eQ4RCByh?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>https://lnkd.in/eQ4RCByh</a></p>

<!---->        
                      <icon class="ellipsis-menu__item-icon text-color-text h-[24px] w-[24px] mr-1" data-delayed-url="https://static.licdn.com/aero-v1/sc/h/iq0x9q37wj214o129ai1yjut">

      </div>

      
            
            
  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">Just out in Harvard Business Review, summary of the Hybrid Experiment results and lessons on how to make hybrid succeed.

Experiment: randomize 1600 graduate employees in marketing, finance, accounting and engineering at <a class="link" href="http://Trip.com?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Trip.com</a> into 5-days a week in office, or 3-days a week in office and 2-days a week WFH. Analyzed 2 years of data.

Two key results
A)  Hybrid and fully-in-office showed no differences in productivity, performance review grade, promotion, learning or innovation.
B) Hybrid had a higher satisfaction rate, and 35% lower attrition. Quit-rate reductions were largest for female employees.

Four managerial lessons
1) Hybrid needs a strong performance management system so managers don’t need to hover over employees at their desks to check their progress. <a class="link" href="http://Trip.com?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Trip.com</a> had an extensive performance review process every six months.

2) Coordinate in-office days at the team or company level. Schedule clarity prevents the frustration of coming to an empty office only to participate in Zoom calls. <a class="link" href="http://Trip.com?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Trip.com</a> coordinated WFH on Wednesday and Friday. 

3) Having leadership buy-in is critical (as with most management practices). <a class="link" href="http://Trip.com?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Trip.com</a>’s CEO and C-suite all support the hybrid policy. 

4) A/B test new policies (as well as products) if possible. Often new policies turn out to be unexpectedly profitable. <a class="link" href="http://Trip.com?trk=keyword-landing-page-text" target="_self" rel="nofollow" data-tracking-control-name="keyword-landing-page-text" data-tracking-will-navigate>Trip.com</a> made millions of dollars more profits from hybrid by cutting expensive turnover.</p>

    </button>

  <div class="attributed-text-segment-list__container relative mt-1 mb-1.5 babybear:mt-0 babybear:mb-0.5">
    <p class="attributed-text-segment-list__content text-color-text !text-sm whitespace-pre-wrap break-words
        attributed-text-segment-list__content-truncated" dir="ltr" data-test-id="main-feed-activity-card__commentary">A few years ago, I was in a high stakes meeting with colleagues from Japan.
I presented my points confidently, thinking I was making a great impression.
But as I scanned the room, I saw blank expressions. 

No nods. No engagement. Just silence.

I panicked. Had I said something wrong? Was my idea unconvincing?

After the meeting, one of my Japanese colleagues pulled me aside and said, “Sumit, we really want to understand you, but you speak too fast.”

That was my light bulb moment.

For years, I assumed that mastering English and business communication was enough to build strong global relationships.

But the real challenge wasn’t just the language - it was the rate of speech!
Most of us don’t realize that speaking speed varies drastically across cultures. 

Here’s an eye-opener:
·      In India, we typically speak at 120–150 words per minute.
·      The global standard for clear communication is around 60–80 words per minute.
·      In Japan, where English is not the first language, this rate drops even further.

So, what happens when we, as fast speakers, communicate with someone who is used to a much slower pace?

Our words blur together. The listener struggles to process. And instead of making an impact, we create confusion.

We often assume that if people don’t understand us, we need to repeat ourselves.

But the truth is, we don’t need to repeat - we need to slow down, simplify, and pause.

If you work in a multicultural environment, here are three things that can dramatically improve your communication:
a.   Control your pace: Consciously slow down when speaking to an international audience. What feels “normal” to you might be too fast for them.
b.   Use simple language: Smaller sentences. Easier words (vocabulary).
c.    Pause &amp; check for understanding: Don’t assume silence means agreement. Ask, “Does that make sense?” or “Would you like me to clarify anything?”

I’ve seen professionals struggle in global roles - not because they lack expertise, but because they fail to adjust their communication style to their audience.

I’ve also seen leaders who thrive across cultures, simply because they master the art of respectful, clear, and paced communication.

If you want to succeed in a global workplace, rate of speech is not just a skill - it’s a strategy.

Have you ever faced challenges due to differences in speaking speed? Let’s discuss.

          <li class="language-selector__item" role="presentation">
            <!-- Adding aria-label to both the li and the button because screen reader focus goes to button on desktop and li on mobile-->
            <button aria-label="Türkçe (土耳其语)" class="font-sans text-xs link block py-[5px] px-2 w-full hover:cursor-pointer hover:bg-color-action hover:text-color-text-on-dark focus:bg-color-action focus:text-color-text-on-dark
                language-selector__link !font-regular" data-tracking-control-name="language-selector-tr_TR" data-locale="tr_TR" role="menuitem" lang="tr_TR">
                Türkçe (土耳其语)
            </button>
          </li>
          <li class="language-selector__item" role="presentation">
            <!-- Adding aria-label to both the li and the button because screen reader focus goes to button on desktop and li on mobile-->
            <button aria-label="Українська (乌克兰语)" class="font-sans text-xs link block py-[5px] px-2 w-full hover:cursor-pointer hover:bg-color-action hover:text-color-text-on-dark focus:bg-color-action focus:text-color-text-on-dark
                language-selector__link !font-regular" data-tracking-control-name="language-selector-uk_UA" data-locale="uk_UA" role="menuitem" lang="uk_UA">
                Українська (乌克兰语)
            </button>
          </li>
          <li class="language-selector__item" role="presentation">
            <!-- Adding aria-label to both the li and the button because screen reader focus goes to button on desktop and li on mobile-->
            <button aria-label="Tiếng Việt (越南语)" class="font-sans text-xs link block py-[5px] px-2 w-full hover:cursor-pointer hover:bg-color-action hover:text-color-text-on-dark focus:bg-color-action focus:text-color-text-on-dark
                language-selector__link !font-regular" data-tracking-control-name="language-selector-vi_VN" data-locale="vi_VN" role="menuitem" lang="vi_VN">
                Tiếng Việt (越南语)
            </button>
          </li>
          <li class="language-selector__item" role="presentation">
            <!-- Adding aria-label to both the li and the button because screen reader focus goes to button on desktop and li on mobile-->
            <button aria-label="已选择简体中文 (简体中文)" class="font-sans text-xs link block py-[5px] px-2 w-full hover:cursor-pointer hover:bg-color-action hover:text-color-text-on-dark focus:bg-color-action focus:text-color-text-on-dark
                language-selector__link--selected" data-tracking-control-name="language-selector-zh_CN" data-locale="zh_CN" role="menuitem" lang="zh_CN">
                <strong>简体中文 (简体中文)</strong>
            </button>
          </li>
          <li class="language-selector__item" role="presentation">
            <!-- Adding aria-label to both the li and the button because screen reader focus goes to button on desktop and li on mobile-->
            <button aria-label="正體中文 (繁体中文)" class="font-sans text-xs link block py-[5px] px-2 w-full hover:cursor-pointer hover:bg-color-action hover:text-color-text-on-dark focus:bg-color-action focus:text-color-text-on-dark
                language-selector__link !font-regular" data-tracking-control-name="language-selector-zh_TW" data-locale="zh_TW" role="menuitem" lang="zh_TW">
                正體中文 (繁体中文)
            </button>
          </li>
<!---->      
        </ul>

          
        <button class="language-selector__button select-none relative pr-2 font-sans text-xs font-bold text-color-text-low-emphasis hover:text-color-link-hover hover:cursor-pointer focus:text-color-link-focus focus:outline-dotted focus:outline-1" aria-expanded="false" data-tracking-control-name="footer-lang-dropdown_trigger">
          <span class="language-selector__label-text mr-0.5 break-words">
            语言
          </span>
          <icon class="language-selector__label-chevron w-2 h-2 absolute top-0 right-0" data-delayed-url="https://static.licdn.com/aero-v1/sc/h/cyolgscd0imw2ldqppkrb84vo"></icon>
        </button>
      
    </div>
  
  
          
  </li>

      </ul>
